metrics = {'T_median': 60.0, 'T_median_ok': False, 'T_median_ratio': 1.9330655957161982, 'contaminated_files': {}, ...}
submitted_data_stats = {'T_max': 60, 'T_mean': 60.0, 'T_median': 60.0, 'T_min': 60, ...}
reference_data_stats = {'chance_majority': {'licking': 0.9588318123909042, 'position': 0.25723790510097916, 'running_speed': 0.25029903335979...y': {'T_max': 238, 'T_mean': 32.62721547677432, 'T_median': 31.0387811634349, 'T_min': 11, ...}, 'device': 'cuda', ...}

    def test_data_stats(metrics, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):
        """Check the converted dataset matches the reference in size, variables and ranges.
    
        Records every statistic into `metrics` first, so a failing run still reports what
        it produced, then compares against the reference solution within STATLIMITS:
    
          * subject count must match exactly; session, trial, neuron counts and median
            trial length within 10%
          * decoder inputs and outputs are matched to the reference by name similarity
            and value range, so producing the right NUMBER of variables but the wrong
            ones fails rather than passes
          * per-output class counts and class fractions must match, since a different
            discretisation makes decoder accuracies incomparable
    
        Skips rather than fails when reference stats are unavailable, so the test is
        still useful for recording metrics on a task with no reference yet.
        """
    
        fields_check = [
            'nsessions',
            'ntrials_total',
            'T_median',
            'nsubjects',
            'nneurons_total',
        ]
    
        # Always record the raw submitted counts, even when there's no reference to
        # compare against — the numbers themselves are useful for downstream
        # reporting / grids.
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field] = submitted_data_stats[field]
    
        # Record per-variable n_classes for the agent's outputs. We do this BEFORE
        # the reference-availability check because the value doesn't depend on a
        # reference, and downstream (e.g. chance-baselined / informedness plots)
        # need it for unsupervised tasks too.
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats.get('output_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_output_range.items():
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{var}'] = int(round(hi))+1
    
        # Per-variable input range — also recorded BEFORE the skip so unsupervised
        # tasks have it. Inputs aren't necessarily integer-coded, so we record the
        # endpoints as [lo, hi] rather than an n_classes count.
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats.get('input_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_input_range.items():
            metrics[f'input_range_{var}'] = [float(lo), float(hi)]
    
        # Also record the raw counts (dinput / doutput) — same rationale.
        metrics['dinput']  = int(submitted_data_stats.get('dinput', 0))
        metrics['doutput'] = int(submitted_data_stats.get('doutput', 0))
    
        if reference_data_stats is None:
            pytest.skip("Reference data stats not available for comparison")
            return
    
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field+'_ratio'] = submitted_data_stats[field] / reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]
            # Verdict recorded where the threshold is applied; the assert below reads it back.
            # The assert loop bails on its first failure, so computing these here is what makes
            # a later field's result knowable after an earlier one has failed.
            metrics[field+'_ok'] = bool(
                ratio_within_limits(metrics[field+'_ratio'], STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']))
    
        # Seed a per-variable verdict for every REFERENCE output, so a variable the agent never
        # produced -- and which therefore never appears in output_matches below -- counts as a
        # miss rather than shrinking the denominator of the fraction categories.
        reference_output_names = list(reference_data_stats['data_summary'].get('output_range') or [])
        for ref_name in reference_output_names:
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
    
        # check that input ranges match via 1-1 matching
        input_matches, input_match_costs = match_variables_by_hungarian(
            submitted_data_stats['input_range'],
            reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range'],
            weight_semantic=.95,
            weight_range=.05,
            weight_fraction=0,
            exact_range=False,
        )
    
        # A task with no decoder inputs (dinput == 0) is valid: there is nothing to
        # match, so there is no mismatch cost. np.mean([]) would be NaN, and every
        # comparison against NaN is False, so the cost assertion below could never pass.
        # Named for what it is: the mean Hungarian MATCHING cost, which is dominated
        # by name similarity and is forced to 0 for exactly-matching names before any
        # range term is computed. It is not a mean of the input_range_error_<var>
        # fields -- that is mean_input_range_error, recorded below.
        mean_input_cost = float(np.mean(input_match_costs)) if len(input_match_costs) else 0.0
        metrics['input_match_mean_cost'] = mean_input_cost
        metrics['input_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in input_matches
        ]
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats['input_range']
        ref_input_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in input_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_input_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_input_range[sub_name]
            scale = abs(ref_hi - ref_lo)
            if scale < 1e-6 and abs(sub_hi - sub_lo) < 1e-6:
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = 0.0
            else:
                scale = max(scale, 1e-6)
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo)/scale, abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)/scale))
        mean_input_range_error = np.mean([metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] for ref_name, _, _ in input_matches]) if len(input_matches) else 0.0
        metrics['mean_input_range_error'] = mean_input_range_error
        # Recorded ONLY when there is something to match. A task with no decoder inputs
        # (allen2p: dinput == 0, input_range == {}) has no input ranges to compare, so leaving
        # the key unset scores the category undefined and drops it from the mean -- rather than
        # awarding a free 1.0 for a check that never happened. Matches undefined_categories()
        # in lesion_analysis.py, which singles out this same case. The assert below is guarded
        # by the same condition, so it never reads a key that was not written.
        if len(input_matches):
            metrics['input_range_ok'] = bool(
                mean_input_range_error <= STATLIMITS['input_range_error'])
    
        # match outputs based on range, fraction, and semantic similarity of names
        output_matches, output_match_costs = match_outputs(
            submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats
        )
        metrics['output_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in output_matches
        ]
    
        # As above: the mean matching cost, not a mean of output_fraction_error_<var>.
        mean_output_match_cost = float(np.mean(output_match_costs))
        metrics['output_match_mean_cost'] = mean_output_match_cost
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats['output_range']
        ref_output_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_output_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_output_range[sub_name]
            metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(abs(sub_hi - ref_hi))
            # Record the number of output classes per variable, so consumers can
            # derive chance level (= 1 / n_classes) for chance-baselined metrics.
            # For integer-coded categorical outputs, n_classes = hi + 1.
            # `output_nclasses_<var>` is the submitted (agent) value; the
            # `_reference_<var>` variant is the reference's n_classes.
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{ref_name}'] = int(round(sub_hi) + 1)
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_reference_{ref_name}'] = int(round(ref_hi) + 1)
            # fraction error per output
            ref_fracs = sorted(reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_fractions'][ref_name].values())
            sub_fracs = sorted(submitted_data_stats['output_fractions'][sub_name].values())
            if len(ref_fracs) == len(sub_fracs):
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = float(np.sum(np.abs(np.array(ref_fracs) - np.array(sub_fracs))))
            else:
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = None
    
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] <= STATLIMITS['output_range_error'])
            # A None error means the class counts differ, so there is no comparable fraction
            # vector. That scores as a miss, but is NOT asserted on below -- the nclasses check
            # already fails that case, and asserting here too would change what the suite
            # rejects. Verdict and assert diverge only here, deliberately.
            frac_error = metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}']
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                frac_error is not None and frac_error <= STATLIMITS['output_fraction_error'])
    
        for field in fields_check:
>           assert metrics[field+'_ok'], f"{field} in submitted data ({submitted_data_stats[field]}) is not within {STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']*100}% of reference ({reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]})"

First, statistics of the converted data and decoder accuracy will be compared to those of an expert-written solution. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n    23→\n    24→## Reference Information\n    25→\n    26→- The reference **paper** \"Unsupervised pretraining in biological neural networks\" is in the file `/app/paper.pdf`.\n    27→- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`. \n    28→- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `/app/code`.\n    29→- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `/app/data`.\n    30→\n    31→## Decoder Task\n    32→\n    33→Temporally aligned based on trial start (corridor entry).\n    34→\n    35→### Decoder Inputs:\n    36→- Time to sound cue, continuous, time-varying\n    37→- Day of training, continuous, per-trial\n    38→- Time since trial start, continuous, time varying\n    39→- Reward availability: 1 if in rewarded corridor, 0 if not, discrete, per-trial\n    40→\n    41→### Decoder Outputs\n    42→- Visual stimulus category. e.g. circle, leaf, etc., per-trial\n    43→- Licking, binary, time-varying. 0 = not licking, 1 = licking\n    44→- Position in corridor discretized into 4 equal-length, 1-m-long spatial bins, time varying\n    45→- Running speed discretized into 4 bins, each corresponding to 25% of the data\n    46→\n    47→## Target Data Format\n    48→\n    49→- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n    50→- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n    51→\n    52→```python\n    53→data = {\n    54→    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n    55→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    56→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    57→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    58→            ...\n    59→        ],\n    60→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    61→            ...\n    62→        ],\n    63→        ...\n    64→    ],\n    65→\n    66→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    67→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    68→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    69→            ...\n    70→        ],\n    71→        ...\n    72→    ],\n    73→\n    74→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    75→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    76→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    77→            ...\n    78→        ],\n    79→        ...\n    80→    ],\n    81→\n    82→    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    83→    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n    84→\n    85→    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    86→    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n    87→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    88→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n    89→\n    90→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    91→    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    92→    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n    93→        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n    94→        ...\n    95→    ],\n    96→\n    97→    'metadata': {\n    98→        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n    99→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n   100→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n   101→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   102→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   103→        ...\n   104→    }\n   105→}\n   106→```\n   107→\n   108→### Key Format Requirements\n   109→\n   110→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   111→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   112→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   113→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   114→\n   115→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   116→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   117→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   118→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   119→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   120→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   121→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   122→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   123→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   124→\n   125→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   126→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   127→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   128→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   129→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   130→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   131→\n   132→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   133→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   134→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   135→\n   136→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   137→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   138→\n   139→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   140→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   141→\n   142→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   143→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   144→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   145→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   146→\n   147→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   148→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   149→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   150→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   151→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   152→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   153→\n   154→## Python environment\n   155→\n   156→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   157→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   158→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   159→\n   160→## Decoder Reference\n   161→\n   162→- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   163→- Optional arguments:\n   164→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   165→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   166→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   167→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   168→\n   169→### Purpose\n   170→\n   171→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   172→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   173→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   174→3. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n   227→\n   228→**Goal**: Understand how the original authors loaded and processed their data.\n   229→\n   230→**Actions**:\n   231→- Look for documentation or README files in the `/app/code` directory\n   232→- Read the provided reference code bases to see how the reference paper loaded and manipulated the data\n   233→- Understand which functions are being called to read, curate, process, and manipulate the data\n   234→- For neural imaging data, does delta F over F need to be computed? \n   235→- For electrophysiology neural data, do cells need to be filtered based on quality? \n   236→- Note to file important functions, their inputs, and outputs\n   237→\n   238→**Done when**: You have documented key functions and their purposes in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 1\".\n   239→\n   240→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 2, update Step 1 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   241→\n   242→---\n   243→\n   244→### Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n   245→\n   246→**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at data in the `/app/data` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n   247→\n   248→**Goal**: Understand the native data structure\n   249→\n   250→**Actions**:\n   251→- Identify how data is organized (all files, formats, hierarchies)\n   252→- Determine available variables and their meanings\n   253→- Check data dimensions and data types\n   254→- Look for documentation or README files\n   255→- Note the data organization and structure\n   256→- Note dataset size parameters such as the **total number** of subjects (mice), sessions, trials, and neurons\n   257→\n   258→**Done when**: You have documented the data structure, file formats, size, and available variables in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 2\".\n   259→\n   260→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 3, update Step 2 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   261→\n   262→---\n   263→\n   264→### Step 3: Reference Text Reading\n   265→\n   266→**Goal**: Extract key information from the paper and methods.\n   267→\n   268→**Actions**:\n   269→- Scan the reference papers and methods.txt for important details about the data\n   270→- Look for and note to file:\n   271→  - Any information about dataset size (number of neurons, subjects, sessions, trials)\n   272→  - Fraction of data with different values, e.g. fraction of rewarded outcomes\n   273→  - Data processing, curation, and filtering\n   274→  - Variables important to experiments\n   275→  - Temporal alignment\n   276→  - Temporal binning\n   277→- Read the reference paper to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\n   278→\n   279→**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 3\".\n   280→\n   281→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   282→\n   283→---\n   284→\n   285→### Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   286→\n   287→**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \n   288→\n   289→**Actions**:\n   290→- Compare your understanding of:\n   291→  - Important functions from exploring the reference code\n   292→  - Dataset structure from exploring the dataset\n   293→  - Analysis described in the reference texts\n   294→- Note and investigate any discrepancies\n   295→- In particular, look for discrepancies in:\n   296→  - Experimental and behavioral variables\n   297→  - Data processing\n   298→  - Data curation\n   299→- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\n   300→\n   301→**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 4\".\n   302→\n   303→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   304→\n   305→---\n   306→\n   307→### Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   308→\n   309→**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\n   310→\n   311→**Actions**:\n   312→- Map source data elements to target format components:\n   313→  - Which variables contain neural activity?\n   314→  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\n   315→  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   316→  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   317→  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   318→  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\n   319→  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `/app/code` directory. \n   320→- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\n   321→  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\n   322→  - Temporal alignment of trials\n   323→  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\n   324→  - Brain regions recorded from\n   325→- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\n   326→- Check for variables indicating valid data periods — exclude invalid data\n   327→- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\n   328→- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\n   329→\n   330→**Done when**:\n   331→- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \"Step 5\"\n   332→- You have documented all decisions under \"Step 5\"\n   333→- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \"Step 5\"\n   334→\n   335→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   336→\n   337→---\n   338→\n   339→### Step 6: Script Development\n   340→\n   341→**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `/app/convert_data.py`.\n   342→\n   343→**Actions**:\n   344→- Write all conversion code in the file `/app/convert_data.py`\n   345→- Script should run as **`python -u /app/convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\n   346→- Include options:\n   347→  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   348→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   349→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   350→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   351→- Write efficient code:\n   352→  - Vectorize loops\n   353→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   354→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   355→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   356→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   357→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   358→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   359→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   360→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   361→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   362→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   363→\n   364→**Done when**: `/app/convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   365→\n   366→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `/app/convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   367→\n   368→---\n   369→\n   370→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   371→\n   372→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   373→\n   374→**Actions**:\n   375→1. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   376→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   377→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   378→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   379→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   380→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   381→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   382→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   383→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   384→3. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   385→4. Analyze the output file `/app/verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   386→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   387→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   388→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   389→\n   390→**Done when**: \n   391→- `/app/sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   392→- `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   393→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   394→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   395→\n   396→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `/app/sample_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   397→\n   398→---\n   399→\n   400→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   401→\n   402→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   403→\n   404→**Actions**:\n   405→1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n   406→2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   407→3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\n   408→\n   409→**Investigate any issues**:\n   410→- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\n   411→\n   412→**Done when**: \n   413→- Decoder training completes without errors\n   414→- Loss decreases over epochs\n   415→- Accuracies are above chance\n   416→- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 8\"\n   417→\n   418→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `/app/train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \n   419→\n   420→---\n   421→\n   422→### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\n   423→\n   424→**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\n   425→\n   426→**Actions**:\n   427→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   428→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   429→3. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_full_out.txt`\n   430→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   431→5. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_full_out.txt`. \n   432→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   433→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   434→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `/app/verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   435→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   436→\n   437→**Done when**:\n   438→- `/app/converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   439→- `/app/verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   440→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   441→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   442→\n   443→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `/app/converted_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   444→\n   445→---\n   446→\n   447→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   448→\n   449→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   450→\n   451→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   452→Read through `/app/verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   453→\n   454→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   455→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   456→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   457→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   458→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   459→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   460→- Document your sanity checks to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   461→\n   462→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   463→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   464→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   465→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   466→- Fix any mismatches. \n   467→- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   468→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   469→\n   470→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   471→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \n   472→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   473→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   474→- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   475→\n   476→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   477→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   478→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   479→\n   480→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   481→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   482→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   483→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   484→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   485→\n   486→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   487→\n   488→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   489→\n   490→---\n   491→\n   492→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   493→\n   494→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   495→\n   496→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   497→\n   498→**Actions**:\n   499→1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   500→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   501→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   502→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \n   503→\n   504→**Done when**: \n   505→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   506→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   507→\n   508→---\n   509→\n   510→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   511→\n   512→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   513→\n   514→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   515→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   516→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   517→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   518→\n   519→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\n   520→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\n   521→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\n   522→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   523→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n   524→\n   525→**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n   526→- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n   527→\n   528→**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n   529→1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n   530→2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n   531→3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n   532→4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n   533→5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \n   534→\n   535→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   536→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   537→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   538→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   539→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   540→\n   541→**Report all checks and results** to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n   542→\n   543→**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n   544→\n   545→---\n   546→\n   547→### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n   548→\n   549→**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n   550→\n   551→**Actions**:\n   552→- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n   553→  - Key decisions and rationale\n   554→  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n   555→  - Validation results and tables\n   556→  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n   557→- Create a user-facing `/app/README.md` summarizing:\n   558→  - Dataset description\n   559→  - How to load and use the converted data\n   560→  - Output format specification\n   561→  - Key statistics\n   562→- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `/app/cache/` folder\n   563→- Create `/app/cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n   564→\n   565→**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n   566→\n   567→---\n   568→\n   569→## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n   570→\n   571→**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING WHEN APPLICABLE**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference paper and code, except where the decoder input and output specifications or training a neural decoder require otherwise. You will be assessed on the statistics and decoder accuracy of your converted data, and on whether an expert would consider each of your decisions well justified.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference paper and code repository when applicable. This requires examining the provided paper, code, and data to understand how the data is formatted and processed, and determine how to balance this with the requirements of the new downstream analysis.\n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your work in two ways. First, statistics of the converted data and decoder accuracy will be compared to those of an expert-written solution. Second, each decision you make about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **paper** \"Unsupervised pretraining in biological neural networks\" is in the file `/app/paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`. \n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `/app/code`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `/app/data`.\n\n## Decoder Task\n\nTemporally aligned based on trial start (corridor entry).\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time to sound cue, continuous, time-varying\n- Day of training, continuous, per-trial\n- Time since trial start, continuous, time varying\n- Reward availability: 1 if in rewarded corridor, 0 if not, discrete, per-trial\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Visual stimulus category. e.g. circle, leaf, etc., per-trial\n- Licking, binary, time-varying. 0 = not licking, 1 = licking\n- Position in corridor discretized into 4 equal-length, 1-m-long spatial bins, time varying\n- Running speed discretized into 4 bins, each corresponding to 25% of the data\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder.\n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01VpjgAajNqYctyfYmHUxuAs","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→# Decisions\n     2→\n     3→## 1-a. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n     4→\n     5→i. Everything is read from three directories under `data`: `beh` for the behavior, `spk` for the deconvolved calcium traces and `retinotopy` for the visual area of each neuron. `beh/Imaging_Exp_info.npy` is the master index and is read first, listing every recording grouped by experiment type. A recording is one mouse on one date in one block, and that triple is the session id, which also names the spike file. Each `Beh_<exp_type>.npy` holds the behavior of several sessions keyed by that id, so the conversion loops over behavior files and reads each one once; the spikes and the retinotopy are read per session.\n     6→\n     7→ii. The index, and the session id built from an entry of it:\n     8→```python\n     9→exp_info = np.load(os.path.join(BEH, 'Imaging_Exp_info.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\n    10→session_id = '%s_%s_%s' % (entry['mname'], entry['datexp'], entry['blk'])\n    11→```\n    12→\n    13→The three reads a session needs:\n    14→```python\n    15→behavior = np.load(os.path.join(BEH, beh_file), allow_pickle=True).item()\n    16→planes = np.load(os.path.join(SPK, '%s_neural_data.npy' % session_id), allow_pickle=True).item()\n    17→area = np.load(os.path.join(RETINO, '%s_%s_%s_%s_trans.npz' % (mouse, year, month, day)))['iarea']\n    18→```\n    19→\n    20→iii. The index is reorganized from experiment type into subject and session, one record per recording, which is the form the conversion iterates over.\n    21→\n    22→## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n    23→\n    24→i. The mouse name is taken from `mname` in the index entry and carried on every record, and the records are grouped by it. At assembly the subjects are the sorted unique names, and `subject_idx` holds each session's index into that list: 89 sessions over 19 mice.\n    25→\n    26→ii. The mouse of a record, and the subject list built from it:\n    27→```python\n    28→records.append((entry['mname'], session_id, 'Beh_%s.npy' % exp_type, beh_key))\n    29→```\n    30→```python\n    31→subjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\n    32→sub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n    33→```\n    34→\n    35→iii. The index already names the mouse, so no split has to be derived.\n    36→\n    37→## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n    38→\n    39→i. A session is one mouse on one date in one block, which the index entry gives as `mname`, `datexp` and `blk`. The id built from the three names the spike file and keys the behavior. A recording listed under more than one experiment type is kept only the first time it is seen, leaving 89 sessions.\n    40→\n    41→ii. The id, and the check that keeps each recording once:\n    42→```python\n    43→session_id = '%s_%s_%s' % (entry['mname'], entry['datexp'], entry['blk'])\n    44→if session_id in seen:\n    45→    continue\n    46→seen.add(session_id)\n    47→```\n    48→\n    49→iii. The recording sometimes repeat under different behavior session type. Therefore a recording that is listed under more than one experiment type is kept only the first time it is seen.\n    50→\n    51→## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n    52→\n    53→i. The split into trials is given by the data: every neural frame carries its trial in `ft_trInd`, and the boundaries are listed as frame numbers as well, `StartFr` at corridor entry, `GrayFr` at the grey space and `EndFr` at the exit. Based on the instruction we define the trials and the sequence between entry to the end of texture (4m). The trials are taken as they are, and within each one the frames kept are every frame inside the texture, `ft_CorrSpc`. Nothing is cut from a trial: it runs to the end of its own traversal, so trials differ in length.\n    54→\n    55→ii. The trials are the ones the data declares, `ntrials` of them, indexed by `ft_trInd`:\n    56→```python\n    57→frames = [trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames) for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])]\n    58→```\n    59→\n    60→and the frames of each are the ones it labels with that trial:\n    61→```python\n    62→inside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\n    63→return np.flatnonzero(inside)\n    64→```\n    65→\n    66→iii. Trial goes from start to either gray or end frame, as described in the paper. As neural decoders generally make use of temporally contiguous data, we do not drop frames in the middle of trials. \n    67→\n    68→## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n    69→\n    70→i. Two trials are dropped: one left with no frames once the behavior is cut to the frames that were imaged, and one longer than the 99th percentile of traversals across the whole dataset. Nothing else is filtered.\n    71→\n    72→ii. The two conditions, and the limit they use:\n    73→```python\n    74→trials = [trial for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])\n    75→          if 0 < frames[trial].size <= limit]\n    76→```\n    77→```python\n    78→lengths = get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir)\n    79→return np.percentile(lengths, TRIAL_LENGTH_PERCENTILE), lengths\n    80→```\n    81→\n    82→iii. The longest traversals are not slow runs, they are animals that stopped. A trial past the 99th percentile is 95% stationary, against 4% for a typical trial, so the distribution separates rather than shading off. The extreme case, trial 391 of `TX88_2022_07_19_1`, runs 5607 frames: the mouse enters, reaches 1.4 m of the 4 m corridor, stands there for 29 minutes and then runs out, contributing 5571 bins that all carry the same position label. Dropping such a trial whole is preferred to shortening it, which would keep a few hundred bins of a parked animal and invent an endpoint the traversal never had. The threshold is measured from the data rather than written down, over every session even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample run and a full one agree about which trials are too long; it comes out at 238.9 frames, 75 s, about ten times the median traversal, and removes 382 trials of 38110. No session loses more than 9.5% of its trials and none falls under the two needed to evaluate a decoder.\n    83→\n    84→## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n    85→\n    86→i. From `spks` in `spk/<session_id>_neural_data.npy`, which is a list of one neurons by frames array per imaging plane, concatenated into a single array. The visual area of each neuron comes from `iarea` in `retinotopy/<mouse>_<date>_trans.npz`.\n    87→\n    88→ii. The two reads and the concatenation:\n    89→```python\n    90→planes = np.load(os.path.join(SPK, '%s_neural_data.npy' % session_id), allow_pickle=True).item()\n    91→spikes = np.concatenate(planes['spks'], 0)\n    92→area = np.load(os.path.join(RETINO, '%s_%s_%s_%s_trans.npz' % (mouse, year, month, day)))['iarea']\n    93→```\n    94→\n    95→iii. The neural data is read directly, nothing is derived from it.\n    96→\n    97→## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n    98→\n    99→i. The traces are not processed. The columns belonging to a trial are taken and the result is stored as float16. The trials are variable length and are left that way, so nothing is padded.\n   100→\n   101→ii. One trial of neural data:\n   102→```python\n   103→neural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n   104→```\n   105→\n   106→iii. The file already holds deconvolved traces, which the methods state every analysis was based on, so no dF/F or deconvolution is needed. float16 rather than float32 because the dataset is quite big. \n   107→\n   108→## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n   109→\n   110→i. The only filter is the visual area. A neuron is kept if its `iarea` falls in V1, mHV, lHV or aHV, and dropped otherwise, which keeps 4,105,393 of 4,691,034 neurons.\n   111→\n   112→ii. The area of each neuron, and the neurons kept:\n   113→```python\n   114→region = np.full(area.size, -1)\n   115→for index, name in enumerate(BRAIN_REGIONS):\n   116→    region[np.isin(area, AREAS[name])] = index\n   117→\n   118→keep = region >= 0\n   119→return spikes[keep], region[keep]\n   120→```\n   121→\n   122→iii. The authors already curated the neurons with the Suite2p cell classifier, and the reference code applies no further filter, so nothing is done beyond assigning the area.\n   123→\n   124→## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n   125→\n   126→i. The alignment event is corridor entry. Trials are of variable length, because mice run at different speeds, and they are stored at that length: every trial starts at its own corridor entry and ends where its traversal ends. Nothing is cut and nothing is padded.\n   127→\n   128→ii. Every frame of the traversal, from corridor entry, and the trial built from exactly those frames:\n   129→```python\n   130→inside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\n   131→return np.flatnonzero(inside)\n   132→```\n   133→```python\n   134→neural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n   135→```\n   136→\n   137→iii. The format does not need one length for every trial. It asks that the bin *size* be the same across trials and sessions, which it is at 315 ms, and it accepts `None` for `off_end` when the trials share no common end. The decoder reads each trial's length off its own array and treats every bin as an independent sample, so a shared window buys nothing. A fixed window costs a great deal: at 32 frames it discarded 435407 real frames, 31.7% of everything inside a corridor, and replaced them with 281757 padding bins, 23.1% of what was written. Trials keep their own length instead, and the small number that are too long to be a traversal at all are dropped whole, as described in 1-e.\n   138→\n   139→## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n   140→\n   141→i. It is not binned or resampled. The imaging frames are the bins: one column of `spks` per frame at 3.17 Hz, so a bin is 315 ms, and a trial holds as many of them as its traversal lasted, a median of 23.\n   142→\n   143→ii. The rate, and the bin size it gives the metadata:\n   144→```python\n   145→FRAME_RATE = 3.17                # imaging rate, from the reference\n   146→```\n   147→```python\n   148→'time_bin_size': 1000.0 / FRAME_RATE,        # ms\n   149→```\n   150→\n   151→iii. The imaging frame is the finest resolution the data has, and every behavior stream is already on the same grid, so there is nothing to resample.\n   152→\n   153→## 3-a. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. Everything is read from three directories under `data`: `beh` for the behavior, `spk` for the deconvolved calcium traces and `retinotopy` for the visual area of each neuron. `beh/Imaging_Exp_info.npy` is the master index and is read first, listing every recording grouped by experiment type. A recording is one mouse on one date in one block, and that triple is the session id, which also names the spike file. Each `Beh_<exp_type>.npy` holds the behavior of several sessions keyed by that id, so the conversion loops over behavior files and reads each one once; the spikes and the retinotopy are read per session.\n\nii. The index, and the session id built from an entry of it:\n```python\nexp_info = np.load(os.path.join(BEH, 'Imaging_Exp_info.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\nsession_id = '%s_%s_%s' % (entry['mname'], entry['datexp'], entry['blk'])\n```\n\nThe three reads a session needs:\n```python\nbehavior = np.load(os.path.join(BEH, beh_file), allow_pickle=True).item()\nplanes = np.load(os.path.join(SPK, '%s_neural_data.npy' % session_id), allow_pickle=True).item()\narea = np.load(os.path.join(RETINO, '%s_%s_%s_%s_trans.npz' % (mouse, year, month, day)))['iarea']\n```\n\niii. The index is reorganized from experiment type into subject and session, one record per recording, which is the form the conversion iterates over.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n\ni. The mouse name is taken from `mname` in the index entry and carried on every record, and the records are grouped by it. At assembly the subjects are the sorted unique names, and `subject_idx` holds each session's index into that list: 89 sessions over 19 mice.\n\nii. The mouse of a record, and the subject list built from it:\n```python\nrecords.append((entry['mname'], session_id, 'Beh_%s.npy' % exp_type, beh_key))\n```\n```python\nsubjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\nsub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n```\n\niii. The index already names the mouse, so no split has to be derived.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. A session is one mouse on one date in one block, which the index entry gives as `mname`, `datexp` and `blk`. The id built from the three names the spike file and keys the behavior. A recording listed under more than one experiment type is kept only the first time it is seen, leaving 89 sessions.\n\nii. The id, and the check that keeps each recording once:\n```python\nsession_id = '%s_%s_%s' % (entry['mname'], entry['datexp'], entry['blk'])\nif session_id in seen:\n    continue\nseen.add(session_id)\n```\n\niii. The recording sometimes repeat under different behavior session type. Therefore a recording that is listed under more than one experiment type is kept only the first time it is seen.\n\n## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. The split into trials is given by the data: every neural frame carries its trial in `ft_trInd`, and the boundaries are listed as frame numbers as well, `StartFr` at corridor entry, `GrayFr` at the grey space and `EndFr` at the exit. Based on the instruction we define the trials and the sequence between entry to the end of texture (4m). The trials are taken as they are, and within each one the frames kept are every frame inside the texture, `ft_CorrSpc`. Nothing is cut from a trial: it runs to the end of its own traversal, so trials differ in length.\n\nii. The trials are the ones the data declares, `ntrials` of them, indexed by `ft_trInd`:\n```python\nframes = [trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames) for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])]\n```\n\nand the frames of each are the ones it labels with that trial:\n```python\ninside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\nreturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n```\n\niii. Trial goes from start to either gray or end frame, as described in the paper. As neural decoders generally make use of temporally contiguous data, we do not drop frames in the middle of trials. \n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two trials are dropped: one left with no frames once the behavior is cut to the frames that were imaged, and one longer than the 99th percentile of traversals across the whole dataset. Nothing else is filtered.\n\nii. The two conditions, and the limit they use:\n```python\ntrials = [trial for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])\n          if 0 < frames[trial].size <= limit]\n```\n```python\nlengths = get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir)\nreturn np.percentile(lengths, TRIAL_LENGTH_PERCENTILE), lengths\n```\n\niii. The longest traversals are not slow runs, they are animals that stopped. A trial past the 99th percentile is 95% stationary, against 4% for a typical trial, so the distribution separates rather than shading off. The extreme case, trial 391 of `TX88_2022_07_19_1`, runs 5607 frames: the mouse enters, reaches 1.4 m of the 4 m corridor, stands there for 29 minutes and then runs out, contributing 5571 bins that all carry the same position label. Dropping such a trial whole is preferred to shortening it, which would keep a few hundred bins of a parked animal and invent an endpoint the traversal never had. The threshold is measured from the data rather than written down, over every session even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample run and a full one agree about which trials are too long; it comes out at 238.9 frames, 75 s, about ten times the median traversal, and removes 382 trials of 38110. No session loses more than 9.5% of its trials and none falls under the two needed to evaluate a decoder.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. From `spks` in `spk/<session_id>_neural_data.npy`, which is a list of one neurons by frames array per imaging plane, concatenated into a single array. The visual area of each neuron comes from `iarea` in `retinotopy/<mouse>_<date>_trans.npz`.\n\nii. The two reads and the concatenation:\n```python\nplanes = np.load(os.path.join(SPK, '%s_neural_data.npy' % session_id), allow_pickle=True).item()\nspikes = np.concatenate(planes['spks'], 0)\narea = np.load(os.path.join(RETINO, '%s_%s_%s_%s_trans.npz' % (mouse, year, month, day)))['iarea']\n```\n\niii. The neural data is read directly, nothing is derived from it.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The traces are not processed. The columns belonging to a trial are taken and the result is stored as float16. The trials are variable length and are left that way, so nothing is padded.\n\nii. One trial of neural data:\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n```\n\niii. The file already holds deconvolved traces, which the methods state every analysis was based on, so no dF/F or deconvolution is needed. float16 rather than float32 because the dataset is quite big. \n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The only filter is the visual area. A neuron is kept if its `iarea` falls in V1, mHV, lHV or aHV, and dropped otherwise, which keeps 4,105,393 of 4,691,034 neurons.\n\nii. The area of each neuron, and the neurons kept:\n```python\nregion = np.full(area.size, -1)\nfor index, name in enumerate(BRAIN_REGIONS):\n    region[np.isin(area, AREAS[name])] = index\n\nkeep = region >= 0\nreturn spikes[keep], region[keep]\n```\n\niii. The authors already curated the neurons with the Suite2p cell classifier, and the reference code applies no further filter, so nothing is done beyond assigning the area.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. The alignment event is corridor entry. Trials are of variable length, because mice run at different speeds, and they are stored at that length: every trial starts at its own corridor entry and ends where its traversal ends. Nothing is cut and nothing is padded.\n\nii. Every frame of the traversal, from corridor entry, and the trial built from exactly those frames:\n```python\ninside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\nreturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n```\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n```\n\niii. The format does not need one length for every trial. It asks that the bin *size* be the same across trials and sessions, which it is at 315 ms, and it accepts `None` for `off_end` when the trials share no common end. The decoder reads each trial's length off its own array and treats every bin as an independent sample, so a shared window buys nothing. A fixed window costs a great deal: at 32 frames it discarded 435407 real frames, 31.7% of everything inside a corridor, and replaced them with 281757 padding bins, 23.1% of what was written. Trials keep their own length instead, and the small number that are too long to be a traversal at all are dropped whole, as described in 1-e.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. It is not binned or resampled. The imaging frames are the bins: one column of `spks` per frame at 3.17 Hz, so a bin is 315 ms, and a trial holds as many of them as its traversal lasted, a median of 23.\n\nii. The rate, and the bin size it gives the metadata:\n```python\nFRAME_RATE = 3.17                # imaging rate, from the reference\n```\n```python\n'time_bin_size': 1000.0 / FRAME_RATE,        # ms\n```\n\niii. The imaging frame is the finest resolution the data has, and every behavior stream is already on the same grid, so there is nothing to resample.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_to_sound_cue* derived from?\n\ni. From `SoundFr`, the frame at which the sound cue was played in each trial, and `ft`, the timestamp of every imaging frame.\n\nii. The frame times of the session, and the cue of every trial placed on them:\n```python\nframe_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\ncue = np.interp(beh['SoundFr'], index, frame_time)\n```\n\niii. The timing is dervied from the frame number.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_to_sound_cue*?\n\ni. The cue frame is interpolated onto the frame time axis, and the input is the cue time minus the time of each bin, in seconds.  Note that here the instruction as *time_to_sound_cue*, so it's positive before the sound cue, and negative after the sound cue.\n\nii. The time axis of the trial, and the input built from it:\n```python\nframe_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\ncue = np.interp(beh['SoundFr'], index, frame_time)\n```\n\n```python\ntime = frame_time[window]\n```\n```python\ncue[trial] - time\n```\n\niii. The time need to be converted to seconds and compute a time difference to the timestamp of each frame.\n\n## 3-c. How is `input` *time_to_sound_cue* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is computed from the frame times of the same `window` used for the neural columns of that trial.\n\nii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n```python\ntime = frame_time[window]\n```\n\niii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *day_of_training* derived from?\n\ni. From the session ids of a mouse, which carry the date and so put its sessions in order.\n\nii. The sessions of a mouse taken in order:\n```python\nfor subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n```\n\n```python\ndef training_days(records):\n    \"\"\"How many days into training each session is, counted per mouse.\"\"\"\n    days, count = {}, {}\n\n    # a session id sorts by date within a mouse, so the sessions are counted in order\n    for subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n        days[session_id] = count.get(subject, 0)\n        count[subject] = days[session_id] + 1\n\n    return days\n```\n\niii. The date string is the only field that orders every session of every mouse, so it is used to put them in order, and the *day_of_training* variable is computed from how many of the training session come before.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *day_of_training*?\n\ni. The day is how many recorded days the mouse is into training, so its first session is 0 and each later one counts up, to at most 7. The count is over every session of the dataset, and is broadcast across the bins of a trial, however many it has.\n\nii. The count, and the broadcast:\n```python\ndef training_days(records):\n    \"\"\"How many days into training each session is, counted per mouse.\"\"\"\n    days, count = {}, {}\n\n    # a session id sorts by date within a mouse, so the sessions are counted in order\n    for subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n        days[session_id] = count.get(subject, 0)\n        count[subject] = days[session_id] + 1\n\n    return days\n```\n\niii. The recording days of a mouse are not consecutive, counting the days it was recorded on gives how far into training it is.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_since_trial_start* derived from?\n\ni. From `StartFr`, the frame at which the mouse entered the corridor on each trial, and `ft`, the timestamp of every imaging frame.\n\nii. The corridor entry of every trial placed on the frame times:\n```python\nstart = np.interp(beh['StartFr'], index, frame_time)\n```\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_since_trial_start*?\n\ni. The entry frame is fractional, so it is interpolated onto the frame time axis, and the input is the time of each bin minus that entry, in seconds, starting near zero. Note that this is *time_since_trial_start* so it is negative before start, postive after.\n\nii. The input built from the trial time axis:\n```python\ntime - start[trial]\n```\n\niii. Simply compute a time difference to the timestamp of each frame.\n\n## 5-c. How is `input` *time_since_trial_start* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. \"\"\"\n    # the master index of every recording, grouped by experiment type\n    exp_info = np.load(os.path.join(datadir, 'beh', 'Imaging_Exp_info.npy'),\n region indices.\n    Returns mask of valid neurons and their region indices.\n    Brain regions: ['V1', 'mHV', 'lHV', 'aHV']\n    \"\"\"\n    region_names = ['V1', 'mHV', 'lHV', 'aHV']\n    n_neurons \"\"\"\n    # Check cache first\n    if session_key in _beh_cache:\n        return _beh_cache[session_key]\n    \n    # Get the experiment types this session belongs to\n    sessions = file\n            for k, v in beh.items():\n                if k not in _beh_cache:\n                    _beh_cache[k] = v\n            if session_key in _beh_cache:\n                return _beh_cache[session_key]\n            # Check for keys with stimtype suffix\n            for k in beh.keys():\n                if k.startswith(session_key):\n                    _beh_cache[session_key]
